Parallel Kac’s Walk Generates PRU
Chuhan Lu, Minglong Qin, Fang Song, Penghui Yao, Mingnan Zhao

Abstract
Ma and Huang recently proved that the PFC construction, introduced by Metger, Poremba, Sinha and Yuen [ 24 ] , gives an adaptive-secure pseudorandom unitary family (PRU). Their proof developed a new path recording technique [ 22 ] . In this work, we show that a linear number of sequential repetitions of the parallel Kac’s Walk , introduced by Lu, Qin, Song, Yao and Zhao [ 21 ] , also forms an adaptive-secure PRU , confirming a conjecture therein. Moreover, it additionally satisfies strong security against adversaries making inverse queries. This gives an alternative PRU construction, and provides another instance demonstrating the power of the path recording technique. We also discuss some further simplifications and implications.



1
Introduction

Pseudorandomness
is a fundamental concept in cryptography. The basic pseudorandom objects, including pseudorandom functions (
PRF
s), pseudorandom permutations (
PRP
s) and pseudorandom generators (
PRG
s), have served as primitives in modern classical cryptography.

Pseudorandom objects in quantum information have witnessed increasing
influences in recent years. The first example is
pseudorandom states

(
PRS
s), introduced by Ji, Liu and Song
[
19
]
, which are a set
of states that can be prepared by polynomial-sized quantum circuits
and look indistinguishable from Haar random states for any
polynomial-time quantum distinguisher. They gave the first
construction of
PRS
s.
PRS
s have found applications in various areas
including quantum cryptography
[
3
]
, quantum learning
theory
[
14
]
and quantum
gravity
[
5
,
31
]
. Since their work, a number of different
constructions have been
discovered
[
7
,
12
,
18
,
6
]
.
Ji, Liu and Song further introduced the concept of
pseudorandom
unitaries
(
PRU
s), which are ensembles of unitaries that are efficient
to implement, but are indistinguishable from Haar random unitaries by
any quantum polynomial-time distinguisher. The construction of
PRU

turns out to be challenging and a large body of work has been
devoted to constructing pseudorandom objects that partially realize

PRU
’s functions. Examples include scalable pseudorandom
states
[
8
]
, pseudorandom function-like quantum state
generators
[
2
]
, pseudorandom isometries
[
1
]
,
and pseudorandom scramblers
[
21
]
.

A giant leap was achieved by Metger, Poremba, Sinha and
Yuen
[
24
]
where a pseudorandom unitary family is constructed
against
non-adaptive
adversaries. Their construction is a PFC
ensemble, where the circuits sequentially apply a uniformly random
Clifford gate, a diagonal unitary with a (pseudorandom) random phase

f:\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}\rightarrow\left\{-1,1\right\}
, and then a (pseudorandom)
permutation matrix on
n
-qubit computational basis states. Soon
after, Ma and Huang in their very recent work
[
22
]
proved that
PFC ensembles are indeed secure against
adaptive
adversaries as well,
provably establishing the feasibility of
PRU
for the first time.

1.1
Main result

In this work, we carry on the exciting advancement on
PRU
lately. We
show that the
parallel Kac’s walk
construction, introduced
in
[
21
]
, also produces an
adaptive-secure

PRU
.
The original
Kac’s walk
,
introduced by Kac
[
20
]
in 1956, is a random walk on unitary
groups, which has been extensively studied by mathematical
physicists. Kac’s walk has also found applications in the construction of
polynomial designs
[
9
]
and memory-optimal dimension
reduction
[
15
]
. To put it in the language of quantum
computing, in one step of Kac’s walk, the algorithm randomly selects two
elements of the computational basis
\left\{\ket{i},\ket{j}\right\}
and implements
a random
2\times 2
unitary in the space spanned by these two basis
elements. Kac’s walk can also be viewed as a random walk on a unit
sphere, where all the random unitaries sampled by the walk are applied
to an initial unit vector sequentially. Pillai and Smith
[
26
]
showed a
tight
\Theta(N\log N)
mixing time for Kac’s walk on an

N
-dimensional unit sphere.

Lu, Qin, Song, Yao and Zhao
[
21
]
introduced a variant
called
parallel Kac’s walk
. It randomly samples a random matching
among all
N
computational basis elements, say

\left\{\left(\ket{i_{1}},\ket{j_{1}}\right),\ldots,\left(\ket{i_{N/2}},\ket{j_{N/2}}\right)\right\}
,
which is done in a single step via a random permutation of all basis elements.
For each pair
\left(\ket{i},\ket{j}\right)
, the algorithm implements a

2\times 2
Haar random unitary. By replacing the random functions and
random permutations with their quantum-secure pseudorandom
counterparts, one obtains an efficient implementation of one step of
the parallel Kac’s walk. It was shown that a parallel Kac’s walk reduces
the mixing time by a factor
N
. Consequently,
O(\log N)
steps of parallel
Kac’s walk, which is linear in the number of qubits
n
, map any input
pure state to a (pseudo)random state.
This is formalized as a primitive called (
pseudo
)
random state scrambler
in
[
21
]
.
It was left open in their paper whether such a construction can generate a
PRU
. In this
paper, we show an affirmative answer that
linear
steps of parallel
Kac’s walk indeed form a
PRU
.

Theorem 1.1

(Main Theorem, Informal)

The distribution of the unitary corresponding to an
O(n)
-step random parallel Kac’s walk is computationally indistinguishable from Haar distribution against adaptive adversaries.
Moreover, without asymptotically increasing the number of steps, it also remains secure against adversaries capable of making inverse queries.

Proof overview.

The proof comprises two phases. First we show that

O(n)
iterations of parallel Kac’s walk effectively project the
adversary’s state to what we term the
distinct block
subspace
. We divide
\{0,1\}^{n}
into
N/2
blocks, each
containing two bit strings. We define
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{t}

to be in the distinct block subspace if they belong to different
blocks. Once the state is promised to reside in the distinct block
subspace, the second phase employs a single step of parallel Kac’s
walk to ensure that the entire construction is indistinguishable from a
Haar random unitary.

More specifically, in the initial phase, we leverage the random state
scrambling property of parallel Kac’s walk as introduced in

[
21
]
to ensure that the adversary’s state is approximately in the

distinct subspace
after
O(n)
steps.
To further show the projection into the distinct block subspace,
imagine inserting an additional random permutation that does not affect the construction
(since every iteration inherently includes a random permutation).
Given that the adversary’s state already resides in the
distinct subspace, applying a random permutation will cause it to fall into the
distinct block subspace with high probability.
Note that 2-designs, such as random Clifford, can also project states onto distinct block subspaces. However, parallel Kac’s walk alone is not known to form a 2-design, and hence this does not help us build a
PRU
purely based on the parallel Kac’s walk. Therefore, we instead directly prove that the parallel Kac’s walk suffices for this purpose by an unexpected application of a random scrambling property established in
[
21
]
.

In the second phase, we adapt the techniques in
[
22
]
. A key ingredient in their method is the
path-recording oracle of
[
22
]
, which was designed so that it behaves indistinguishably from both the (purified)
\mathsf{PF}
and Haar random distribution as long as the queries are restricted to tuples of distinct elements (a.k.a. the distinct subspace). In our case, because of the
2\times 2
block diagonal structure of the random rotations in parallel Kac’s walk, we instead need to work with the distinct block subspace as indicated earlier. We thus design a variant path-recording oracle tailored to the distinct block subspace structure, and show that it serves as an effective bridge to reason about the indistinguishability of one-round parallel Kac’s walk and Haar random unitary when restricting queries in the distinct block subspace. This also illustrates the versatility of the path-recording technique and provides a template for further extension.

1.2
Discussions

As far as we are aware, existing provably-secure constructions of PRU all follow the PFC paradigm. For example, Schuster, Haferkamp and
Huang
[
29
]
developed a “gluing” lemma which can
compose
\omega(\log n)
-qubit
PRU
by a two-layer brickwork into an
n
-qubit
PRU
. However, prior to our construction, the only instantiation of the small
\omega(\log n)
-qubit
PRU
would be by PFC. More recently in
[
11
]
, it was observed that the (pseudo-)random permutation
P
in the PFC construction can be substituted by a two-round Luby-Rackoff Feistel network, and several
PRU
constructions with reduced depth were obtained. This can be seen as a further refinement of the PFC paradigm. Our Kac-walk based PRU hence gives the first secure construction that explores a new route drastically deviating from the PFC style.
1
1

1

An earlier version of
[
4
]
claimed a proof for a variant of a candidate
PRU
proposed in
[
19
]
, but a bug was found and the claim was retracted later.

While our PRU construction does not provide efficiency advantages over
the PFC construction, there are potential benefits in other
regards. First, it is usually valuable to have multiple candidates for
a primitive available, to cater to different use cases and to mitigate
the risk in case of unexpected vulnerabilities in some candidates. It
is also preferable in cryptography, for practical implementation
concerns, to base the construction on as few primitives as
possible. For instance, the popular HMAC instantiates the
Hash-then-MAC paradigm using hash functions only as opposed to the
vanilla instantiation by a hash function and a MAC scheme
separately. We can view each step of the parallel Kac’s walk as a basic
module and then the construction consists of repetitions of this basic
module. Finally, since
PRU
s
are the quantum analogue of pseudorandom permutations (i.e., block
ciphers), our PRU is also reminiscent of the famous Feistel network and variants in practical block ciphers such as AES,
where a basic unit is iterated in multiple rounds to achieve desirable
security properties.

The construction and analysis in our work also shed new light on designing
PRU
and quantum pseudorandomness more broadly. From the perspective of appropriate path-recording oracles, one can argue that
PF
in the PFC construction and one-round parallel Kac’s walk are already indistinguishable from Haar random unitary under respective restrictions on the adversary’s queries. Thus to obtain a full-fledged PRU, we can prepend to it a “filter” (random Clifford/2-design
C
or
O(n)
-round parallel Kac’s walk) to enforce the restriction. Alternatively, we can view both
PF
and one-round parallel Kac’s walk as a “randomness-compiler”: as long as the initial object (random Clifford/2-design
C
in PFC or
O(n)
-round leading parallel Kac’s walk) admits some well-specified “benign” randomizing property (e.g., approximating Haar under some twirling operation), composing with
PF
or one-round parallel Kac’s walk will convert it to a PRU. We distill such claims in the main body, which would be useful in building more
PRU
s and other quantum (pseudo-)random objects. For example, one can immediately obtain a host of new
PRU
constructions, such as an arbitrary 2-design or random-state scrambler followed by one-round parallel Kac’s walk.

Some open questions naturally follow from the above discussion. Can we
reduce the number of rounds of the parallel Kac’s walk, ideally to
constant rounds? This appears difficult with the current analysis, and
new techniques may be needed. Following the analogy we draw between
our construction and classical constructions of block ciphers à
la Feistel network, it is worth exploring quantum analogues of the
wide variations on Feistel network (e.g., unbalanced Luby-Rackoff).
Can our construction be further simplified? Can we replace all i.i.d.
random rotations in one step of parallel Kac’s walk by the same random
rotation? Recent research on orthogonal repeated averaging, which is a
simplified Kac’s walk, alludes to an affirmative answer. The other possible
simplification is replacing the
PRP
s in the construction by random local
permutations, like practical architecture of DES[2]-brickwork circuits.
If both simplifications were plausible, we would obtain a construction of local
random circuits, which is also a
PRU
, answering a longstanding open
problem in quantum complexity theory. On a more technical note,

[
21
]
identifies a strong
dispersing
property of the parallel Kac’s walk.
Does this property carry over and equip our
PRU
with additional properties? This is both
interesting for the sake of Kac’s walk and possible applications of the
resulting
PRU
.
Finally, can Kac’s walk lead to pseudorandom operations on other groups?
In particular, since Kac’s walk also arises on
\mathrm{SO}(N)

[
16
,
23
,
17
,
10
,
25
,
27
,
28
]
,
it would be interesting to investigate
whether it yields other pseudorandom operations, such as
pseudorandom orthogonal operators.

Organization.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
Section

2
introduces the notation and cryptographic preliminaries.
Section

3
proves the projection lemma into the distinct block subspace.
Section

4
presents the parallel Kac’s walk construction and proves its adaptive security.
Section

5
extends the analysis to inverse queries and proves strong security. Deferred proofs are given in the appendix.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notation

Unless stated otherwise, we use
n
to denote the number of qubits and
N=2^{n}
to denote the dimension.
We denote the set of unitaries of dimension
N
by
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
. The symbol
\mu
represents the Haar random distribution over quantum states or unitaries, depending on the context.
For finite sets
\mathcal{X}
and

\mathcal{Y}
, we use
\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{Y}}
to denote the set of
all functions
\{f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}\}
.
We generally refer to the permutation group over elements in set
\mathcal{X}
as
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
. We often write
\mathcal{S}_{N}
instead of

\mathcal{S}_{\{0,1\}^{n}}
to denote the permutation group over elements in

\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
. In the context of unitary matrices,
\mathcal{S}_{N}
refers to the group of permutation unitaries of dimension
N
, and
S\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}
indicates sampling a permutation unitary uniformly at random.
Given two density operators
\rho,\eta
, the trace distance between them is
\mathrm{TD}\left(\rho,\eta\right)=\left\lVert\rho-\eta\right\rVert_{1}
.

For
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
we define

\mathrm{val}\!\left(x\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}2^{-i}x_{i}
and
use
\overline{x}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
to denote the binary string
obtained by flipping the first bit of
x
.
We divide the set
\{0,1\}^{n}
into
2^{n-1}
blocks
according to the suffix of each string.
For any
x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
we say that
x
and
y
belong to the same block
if
x
and
y
share the same suffix of length
n-1

(i.e.,
x_{2}=y_{2},\dots,x_{n}=y_{n}
).
Conversely, we say that
x
and
y
are in
distinct blocks

if they have different suffixes.
For
t\in\mathbb{N}
, we use
\mathsf{DB}_{t}
to denote the set of all
t
-tuples
consisting of strings from distinct blocks. That is,

\mathsf{DB}_{t}\coloneq\left\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})\in(\{0,1\}^{n})^{t}:\forall\ i\neq j,\ x_{i}\text{ and }x_{j}\text{ are in distinct blocks}\right\}.

We also need the following lemmas:

Lemma 1

(
[
22
, Lemma 2.2]
)

Let
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}
be a density matrix on registers
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D}
and let
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}
be a projector of the form
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}=\mathsf{Id}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{C}}}\otimes\Pi^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{D}}}
, where
\Pi^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{D}}}
is a projector that acts on register
\mathsf{D}
. Then

\mathsf{TD}\left(\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{D}}}(\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}),\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{D}}}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}\cdot\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}\right)\right)=1-\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{CD}}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma 2

(Gentle Measurement Lemma
[
22
, Lemma 2.3]
)

Let
\ket{\psi}
be a quantum state,

U_{1},\dots,U_{t}
be unitary operators,
and
\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{t}
be projectors.
We have

\left\lVert U_{t}\cdots U_{1}\ket{\psi}-\Pi_{t}U_{t}\cdots\Pi_{1}U_{1}\ket{\psi}\right\rVert_{2}\leq t\cdot\sqrt{1-\left\lVert\Pi_{t}U_{t}\cdots\Pi_{1}U_{1}\ket{\psi}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

2.2
Adversary with Access to Oracle

We adopt the model for adversaries with oracle access in
[
22
]
.

Definition 1

(Adversary with Access to Oracle)
.

An adversary
\mathcal{A}
with oracle access is a quantum algorithm
which queries an oracle
\mathcal{O}
on its first
n
-qubit register
\mathsf{A}
without knowing the description of
\mathcal{O}
.
The adversary owns an additional
m
-qubit ancillary register, denoted by
\mathsf{B}
.

A
t
-query adversary
\mathcal{A}
with oracle access is specified by a
t
-tuple of unitaries
(A^{(1)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}},\cdots,A^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}})
.
The view of the adversary after all the queries is

\ket{\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}}_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\mathcal{O}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A^{(i)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\kern 5.0pt.

We also allow the adversary
\mathcal{A}
to make both forward queries (i.e., to
\mathcal{O}
) and inverse queries (i.e., to
\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}
).
In this case,
a
t
-query adversary
\mathcal{A}

is specified by a
t
-tuple of unitaries
(A^{(1)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}},\cdots,A^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}})
and a Boolean string
b\in\{0,1\}^{t}
.
The view of the adversary after all the queries is

\ket{\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{O}}_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\left((1-b_{i})\cdot\mathcal{O}+b_{i}\cdot\mathcal{O}^{\dagger}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A^{(i)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Unless otherwise specified,
we assume that the adversary makes
only forward queries.

Definition 2

(Computational Indistinguishability)
.

We say two distributions
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
are computationally indistinguishable if for any
\mathrm{poly}\!\left(n\right)
-time adversary
\mathcal{A}
with oracle access who makes
t=\mathrm{poly}\!\left(n\right)
queries,

\left|\underset{U\sim\mathcal{D}_{1}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\!\left[\mathcal{A}^{U}\text{ outputs }1.\right]-\underset{V\sim\mathcal{D}_{2}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\!\left[\mathcal{A}^{V}\text{ outputs }1.\right]\right|=\mathrm{negl}\!\left(n\right)\kern 5.0pt.

2.3
Relation States

For
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathfrak{R}_{t}
represents the set of
all size-
t
relations

R=\left\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{t},y_{t})\right\}\subseteq\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}\times\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
.
We allow the relations to be a multiset.
And let
\mathfrak{R}\coloneq\cup_{t=0}^{N}\mathfrak{R}_{t}
be the set of
all relations with size at most
N
.
For
R\in\mathfrak{R}_{t}
,
the corresponding
relation state
is defined by

\ket{R}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{XY}}}\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma_{R}}}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{t}}S_{\sigma}\ket{x_{1},\dots,x_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes S_{\sigma}\ket{y_{1},\dots,y_{t}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Y}}}\kern 5.0pt,

(1)

where
S_{\sigma}
is a permutation operator on
\left(\mathbb{C}^{2^{n}}\right)^{\otimes t}
defined as

S_{\sigma}:\ket{x_{1},\dots,x_{t}}\mapsto\ket{x_{\sigma^{-1}(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma^{-1}(t)}}

and the normalizer is given by

\gamma_{R}\coloneq t!\cdot\sum_{x,y\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{t}\delta_{(x_{i},y_{i})=(x,y)}\right)!.

Fact 2.1
.

\left\{\ket{R}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{XY}}}\right\}_{R\in\mathfrak{R}}
forms an orthogonal basis.

We further introduce additional notation for later use.
Let
\mathfrak{R}_{t}^{\mathsf{yDB}}
be the set of all size-
t
relations

R=\left\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{t},y_{t})\right\}
such that

(y_{1},\dots,y_{t})\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}
.
Let
\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{yDB}}:=\cup_{t=0}^{N}\mathfrak{R}_{t}^{\mathsf{yDB}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{R}_{t}^{\mathsf{DB}}
be the set of all size-
t
relations

R=\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{t},

y_{t})\}
such that
both
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{t})

are in
\mathsf{DB}_{t}
.
Let
\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{DB}}:=\cup_{t=0}^{N}\mathfrak{R}_{t}^{\mathsf{DB}}
.
For any relation
R
, define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Dom}\left(R\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x:\exists y\in\{0,1\}^{n}\text{ s.t. }(x,y)\in R\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Im}\left(R\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{y:\exists x\in\{0,1\}^{n}\text{ s.t. }(x,y)\in R\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{BDom}\left(R\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x:\exists y\in\{0,1\}^{n}\text{ s.t. }(x,y)\in R\text{ or }(\bar{x},y)\in R\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathrm{BIm}\left(R\right)

\displaystyle:=\left\{y:\exists x\in\{0,1\}^{n}\text{ s.t. }(x,y)\in R\text{ or }(x,\bar{y})\in R\right\}\,.

2.4
Cryptography

In this section, we will review various definitions and results in
cryptography. Throughout this work,
\lambda
denotes a security
parameter.

Definition 3

(Quantum-Secure Pseudorandom Function)
.

Let
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be the key space, the domain and range, all
implicitly depending on the security parameter
\lambda
. A keyed
family of functions
\left\{\textup{{PRF}}_{k}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}\right\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}}
is a
quantum-secure pseudorandom function (QPRF) if the following two
conditions hold:

1.

Efficient generation
.
\textup{{PRF}}_{k}
is polynomial-time computable on a classical computer.

2.

Pseudorandomness
. For any polynomial-time quantum oracle algorithm
\mathcal{A}
,
\textup{{PRF}}_{k}
with a random
k\leftarrow\mathcal{K}
is indistinguishable from a truly random function
f\leftarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{X}}
in the sense that:

\left|\underset{k\leftarrow\mathcal{K}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\!\left[\mathcal{A}^{\textup{{PRF}}_{k}}\!\left(1^{\lambda}\right)=1\right]-\underset{f\leftarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\mathcal{X}}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\!\left[\mathcal{A}^{f}\!\left(1^{\lambda}\right)=1\right]\right|=\mathrm{negl}\!\left(\lambda\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Definition 4

(Quantum-Secure Pseudorandom Permutation)
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be the key space, and
\mathcal{X}
be both the domain and range,
implicitly depending on the security parameter
\lambda
. A keyed
family of permutations
\left\{\textup{{PRP}}_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}\right\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}}
is a
quantum-secure pseudorandom permutation (QPRP) if the following two
conditions hold:

1.

(
Efficient generation
).
\textup{{PRP}}_{k}
and
\textup{{PRP}}_{k}^{-1}
are polynomial-time
computable on a classical computer.

2.

(
Pseudorandomness
). For any polynomial-time quantum
oracle algorithm
\mathcal{A}
,
\textup{{PRP}}_{k}
with a random
k\leftarrow\mathcal{K}
is
indistinguishable from a truly random permutation
\sigma\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}

in the sense that:

\left|\underset{k\leftarrow\mathcal{K}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\!\left[\mathcal{A}^{\textup{{PRP}}_{k},\textup{{PRP}}_{k}^{-1}}\!\left(1^{\lambda}\right)=1\right]-\underset{\sigma\leftarrow S_{\mathcal{X}}}{\mathrm{Pr}}\!\left[\mathcal{A}^{\sigma,\sigma^{-1}}\!\left(1^{\lambda}\right)=1\right]\right|=\mathrm{negl}\!\left(\lambda\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Under the assumption that post-quantum one-way functions exist,
Zhandry proved the existence of
QPRF
s
[
32
]
.
QPRP
s can be
constructed from
QPRF
s efficiently
[
30
,
33
]
.

Definition 5

(Random State Scrambler
[
21
]
)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
.
We say
\mathcal{D}
is a
random state scrambler distribution
with error
\epsilon
if for any
n
-qubit pure state
\ket{\phi}
and
\ell\in\mathrm{poly}\!\left(n\right)
,

\mathrm{TD}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[K^{\otimes\ell}\ket{\phi}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\phi}^{\otimes\ell}K^{\otimes\ell,\dagger}\right],\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ket{\psi}\in\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\psi}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\psi}^{\otimes\ell}\right]\right)\leq\epsilon\kern 5.0pt.

We call a random unitary
K
sampled from such distribution an
\epsilon
-random state scrambler (
\epsilon
-
RSS
).

Definition 6

(Pseudorandom Unitary Operator)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
.
We say
\mathcal{D}
is a
pseudorandom unitary distribution
(
PRU
) if

•

\mathcal{D}
can be sampled in
\mathrm{poly}\!\left(n\right)
time;

•

\mathcal{D}
is computationally indistinguishable from Haar random over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
.

3
Projecting into Distinct Block Subspace

This section shows that applying an
RSS
operator
followed by a random permutation to any state results in a state
that is mostly within the
distinct block subspace
.
We define the projector onto the distinct block subspace as follows.

Definition 7

(Distinct Block subspaces)
.

Let
0\leq t\leq N
, and let

\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{}}}^{(t)}:=\sum_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}}\ket{x_{1},\dots,x_{t}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x_{1},\dots,x_{t}}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma 3

Let
0\leq t\leq N
,
and let
\mathcal{R}
be a distribution over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
such that
for
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}
,
K^{\dagger}
is an

\epsilon
-
RSS
with
\epsilon=O\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)
.
Define
\mathcal{D}
to be the distribution that samples
G=PK
where

P\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}
is a random permutation unitary and
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}
.
For any state
\rho
on an
nt
-qubit register
\mathsf{X}=\left(\mathsf{X}_{1},...,\mathsf{X}_{t}\right)
together with another arbitrary register,
we have

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot G^{\otimes t}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\rho G^{\otimes t,\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\right)\right]\geq 1-O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

Using the cyclic property of trace and then the definition of
\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{}}}^{(t)}
,

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot G^{\otimes t}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\rho G^{\otimes t,\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\right)\right]=

\displaystyle~\mathrm{Tr}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[G^{\otimes t,\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\cdot\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot G^{\otimes t}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\cdot\rho\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathrm{Tr}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[G^{\otimes t,\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\cdot\sum_{x\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}}\ket{x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\cdot G^{\otimes t}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\cdot\rho\right]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(2)

Now define
\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1},\dots,X_{t}}}}:=\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{-(X_{1},\dots,X_{t})}}}(\rho)
,
where
\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{-(X_{1},\dots,X_{t})}}}
represents tracing out all registers except
{\mathsf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{X}_{t}}
.
Then
Eq.

2
can be rewritten as

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[G^{\otimes t,\dagger}\cdot\sum_{x\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}}\ket{x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x}\cdot G^{\otimes t}\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1},\dots,X_{t}}}}\right]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

This implies:

\displaystyle 1-

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot G^{\otimes t}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\rho G^{\otimes t,\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=~\mathrm{Tr}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[G^{\otimes t,{\dagger}}\cdot\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{nt}\setminus\mathsf{DB}_{t}}\ket{x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x}\cdot G^{\otimes t}\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1},\dots,X_{t}}}}\right]\right)\kern 5.0pt.

(3)

We can see that the set
\{0,1\}^{nt}\setminus\mathsf{DB}_{t}
includes all
t
-tuples where (a)
\exists i\neq j
s.t.
x_{i}=x_{j}

or (b)
\exists\mathrm{i}\neq j
s.t.
x_{i}=\overline{x}_{j}
where
\overline{x}_{j}
flips the first bit of
x_{j}
. We further define two
projectors to capture these two situations:

\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x}\otimes\ket{x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x}\kern 5.0pt,\qquad\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x}\otimes\ket{\bar{x}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\bar{x}}\kern 5.0pt.

And we have

\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{nt}\setminus\mathsf{DB}_{t}}\ket{x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1},\dots,X_{t}}}}\preceq\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq t}\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}+\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\kern 5.0pt,

(4)

where
\preceq
represents the positive semidefinite order;
\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}
is the equality projector on registers
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
;
and
\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}
is the flip-first-bit projector on registers
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
.
Combining
Eq.

3
and the inequality
Eq.

4
:

\displaystyle 1-\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot G^{\otimes t}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\rho G^{\otimes t,\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq t}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(G^{\otimes t,\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}+\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot G^{\otimes t}\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1},\dots,X_{t}}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq t}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(G^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes G^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\left(\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}+\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\left(G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq t}\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}\\
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\begin{aligned} &\left((PK)^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes(PK)^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\left(\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}+\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\right)\\
&\qquad{}\cdot\left(PK_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes PK_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\end{aligned}\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt,

(5)

where the part inside of the summation of
Eq.

5

can be rewritten as the sum of the following two terms:

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}\\
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left((PK)^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes(PK)^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(PK_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes PK_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt,

(6)

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}\\
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left((PK)^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes(PK)^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(PK_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes PK_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\right)\right]\kern 5.0pt.

(7)

We will bound these two terms one by one. First, note that
for any permutation matrix
P
,
(P^{\dagger}\otimes P^{\dagger})\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}\cdot(P\otimes P)=\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle(\ref{DBproj_eq5})=

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}\\
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\left(P^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes P^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(P_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes P_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\left(K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1},\dots,X_{t}}}}\right)\right]\kern 4.62497pt.

Then, since
\sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1},\dots,X_{t}}}}
is a density operator, we have

\displaystyle(\ref{DBproj_eq5})\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}\kern 5.0pt.

By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle(\ref{DBproj_eq5})\leq

\displaystyle\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\leftarrow\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(U^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes U^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(U_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes U_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\Bigg\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\leftarrow\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(U^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes U^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(U_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes U_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{}-\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\right]\Bigg\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\biggl(\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\leftarrow\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(U^{\dagger}\otimes U^{\dagger}\right)\cdot\ket{x,x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x,x}\cdot\left(U\otimes U\right)\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle+\Bigg\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\leftarrow\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(U^{\dagger}\otimes U^{\dagger}\right)\cdot\ket{x,x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x,x}\cdot\left(U\otimes U\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{}-\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(K^{\dagger}\otimes K^{\dagger}\right)\cdot\ket{x,x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x,x}\cdot\left(K\otimes K\right)\right]\Bigg\rVert_{\infty}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left(\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\leftarrow\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(U^{\dagger}\otimes U^{\dagger}\right)\cdot\ket{x,x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x,x}\cdot\left(U\otimes U\right)\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}+O\!\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where we use the property of
O\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)
-
RSS
to derive
the last inequality.
Since
U\ket{x}
is a Haar random state,
we have
\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\leftarrow\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(U^{\dagger}\otimes U^{\dagger}\right)\cdot\ket{x,x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x,x}\cdot\left(U\otimes U\right)\right]=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ket{\psi}\leftarrow\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\psi,\psi}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\psi,\psi}\right]

and the operator norm is
\frac{2}{N(N+1)}

[
13
, Proposition 6]
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle(\ref{DBproj_eq5})\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left(\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ket{\psi}\leftarrow\mu\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\psi,\psi}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\psi,\psi}\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}+O\!\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~N\cdot\left(\frac{2}{N(N+1)}+O\!\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)\right)=O\!\left(N^{-1}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Next, we attempt to bound (
7
):

\displaystyle(\ref{DBproj_eq6})

\displaystyle=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}\\
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\left(P^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes P^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\left(P_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes P_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\left(K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes K_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\rho_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(P^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes P^{\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{ffb}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i},X_{j}}}}\cdot\left(P_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{i}}}}\otimes P_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{j}}}}\right)\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\end{subarray}}\!\left[(P\otimes P)^{\dagger}\cdot\ket{x,\bar{x}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x,\bar{x}}\cdot(P\otimes P)\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left\lVert\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{z\neq y}\ket{z,y}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{z,y}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N-1}\kern 5.0pt.

Since both
Eqs.

6
and
7
are upper bounded by
O\!\left(N^{-1}\right)
, by substituting into
Eq.

5
and using a union bound on all
i
and
j
, we have

1-\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot G^{\otimes t}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\rho G^{\otimes t,\dagger}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\right)\right]\leq O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt.\qed

4
PRU from Parallel Kac’s Walk

In this section, we introduce our construction for PRU which is inspired
by parallel Kac’s walk.
Our construction is simply to repeat the parallel Kac’s walk.

4.1
The
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
distribution

Our construction is based on
parallel Kac’s walk
,
a random walk on unit vectors within Hilbert spaces.
A single step of parallel Kac’s walk can be simulated
by firstly sampling a random function
f:\left\{0,1\right\}^{n-1}\to\left\{0,1\right\}^{3d}

and a random permutation
\sigma:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
,
and then applying two unitary operators
P_{\sigma}
and
H_{f}
in sequence.
The unitary
P_{\sigma}
is the permutation matrix defined by

P_{\sigma}=\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{\sigma(x)}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x}\kern 5.0pt.

The unitary
P_{\sigma}
will pair the
2^{n}
computational basis up
according to their images after the permutation
\sigma
.
More specifically, the basis
\ket{x}
and
\ket{z}
are paired up
iff
\sigma(x)
and
\sigma(z)
share the same suffix of length
n-1
,
and each pair can be identified by its unique suffix.
The unitary
H_{f}
then applies independent
2\times 2
Haar random unitaries
on all pairs in the following way:

1.

for every
y\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}
,
we first parse
f(y)=f_{\alpha}(y)\|f_{\beta}(y)\|f_{\theta}(y)

such that
f_{\alpha}(y),

f_{\beta}(y),

f_{\theta}(y)\in\{0,1\}^{d}
,

2.

calculate three angles

\theta_{y}=\arcsin\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{val}\!\left(f_{\theta}(y)\right)}\right)\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt\alpha_{y}=2\pi\cdot\mathrm{val}\!\left(f_{\alpha}(y)\right)\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt\beta_{y}=2\pi\cdot\mathrm{val}\!\left(f_{\beta}(y)\right)\kern 5.0pt,

3.

apply
U(\alpha_{y},\beta_{y},\theta_{y})=\begin{bmatrix}e^{\mathrm{i}\alpha_{y}}\cos(\theta_{y})&-e^{\mathrm{i}\beta_{y}}\sin(\theta_{y})\\
e^{-\mathrm{i}\beta_{y}}\sin(\theta_{y})&e^{-\mathrm{i}\alpha_{y}}\cos(\theta_{y})\end{bmatrix}
on the pair with suffix
y
.

The expression for
H_{f}
is

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}\!\!\left(\begin{matrix}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{\alpha_{y}+\beta_{y}}{2}\right)}&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{\alpha_{y}+\beta_{y}}{2}\right)}\end{matrix}\right)\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta_{y}&-\sin\theta_{y}\\
\sin\theta_{y}&\cos\theta_{y}\end{pmatrix}\!\!\left(\begin{matrix}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{\alpha_{y}-\beta_{y}}{2}\right)}&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{\alpha_{y}-\beta_{y}}{2}\right)}\end{matrix}\right)\!\!\otimes\ket{y}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{y}\,,

(8)

where
U(\alpha_{y},\beta_{y},\theta_{y})
is decomposed into a product of three matrices.
The unitary
H_{f}
can be approximated
by a polynomial-time implementable unitary
\widehat{H}_{f}

satisfying that
\|H_{f}-\widehat{H}_{f}\|_{\infty}
is sufficiently small
[
21
]
.

Our construction for PRU is simply to repeat the parallel Kac’s walk.
We define two distributions over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
, denoted by
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{HP}}_{n,T}
:

Definition 8
.

\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
is a distribution over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
which can be sampled via the following procedure:

•

sample
T
uniformly random functions

f_{1},\dots,f_{T}:\left\{0,1\right\}^{n-1}\to\left\{0,1\right\}^{3d}
,
and
T
uniformly random permutations
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{T}:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\{0,1\}^{n}
,

•

output the unitary
\mathsf{Kac}=\prod_{i=1}^{T}\left(H_{f_{i}}\cdot P_{\sigma_{i}}\right)
.

Similarly, we define the distribution
\widehat{\mathsf{HP}}_{n,T}
by substituting
H_{f}
with
\widehat{H}_{f}
, and we denote the unitary sampled according to
\widehat{\mathsf{HP}}_{n,T}
as
\widehat{\mathsf{Kac}}
.

These two distributions are indistinguishable by any polynomial-time quantum adversary.

Lemma 4

For
T=\mathrm{poly}\!\left(n\right)
and
d=5n
,
\widehat{\mathsf{HP}}_{n,T}
is computationally indistinguishable from
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
.

Proof.

Consider the views
\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{Kac}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}
and
\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\widehat{\mathsf{Kac}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}
of a
t
-query adversary
\mathcal{A}
with oracle access to
\mathsf{Kac}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{Kac}}
, respectively.
It is sufficient to show that the trace distance between these two states is negligible.
To this end, we define the following hybrids: for
0\leq j\leq t
,

\ket{\varphi_{j}}=\prod_{k=1}^{j}\left(\widehat{\mathsf{Kac}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A^{(i)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\right)\prod_{j=k+1}^{t}\cdot\left(\mathsf{Kac}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A^{(i)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\kern 5.0pt.

It is evident that

\ket{\varphi_{0}}=\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{Kac}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}
and

\ket{\varphi_{t}}=\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\widehat{\mathsf{Kac}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}
, and
the trace distance between two adjacent hybrids is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\lVert\ket{\varphi_{j}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\varphi_{j}}-\ket{\varphi_{j+1}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\varphi_{j+1}}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\prod_{i=1}^{T}\left(H_{f_{i}}\cdot P_{\sigma_{i}}\right)-\prod_{i=1}^{T}\left(\widehat{H}_{f_{i}}\cdot P_{\sigma_{i}}\right)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{T}\|H_{f_{i}}-\widehat{H}_{f_{i}}\|_{\infty}\leq 8\pi\cdot 2^{-d}\cdot T=\mathrm{negl}\!\left(n\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where the second inequality is from the triangle inequality and the last inequality is from Lemma 19 in
[
21
]
.
Thus, we have that by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Kac}\leftarrow\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{Kac}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{Kac}}}\right]-\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\mathsf{Kac}}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathsf{HP}}_{n,T}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\widehat{\mathsf{Kac}}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\widehat{\mathsf{Kac}}}}\right]\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq~

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\varphi_{j}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\varphi_{j}}\right]-\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\varphi_{j+1}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\varphi_{j+1}}\right]\right\rVert_{1}\leq t\cdot 8\pi\cdot 2^{-d}\cdot T=\mathrm{negl}\!\left(n\right)\kern 5.0pt.\qed

It is proved in
[
21
]
that with large enough
T
and
d
, the
distribution
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
is an
RSS
distribution. Formally, we have
the following theorem by adjusting the parameters used in
[
21
, Theorem 10]
.

Theorem 4.1

Let
T=30n
and
d=5n
.
Then the distribution
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
is an
\epsilon
-
RSS
distribution on
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
with
\epsilon=O\!\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)
.
Moreover, if
\mathsf{Kac}\leftarrow\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
, then
\mathsf{Kac}^{\dagger}
is also an
\epsilon
-
RSS
with
\epsilon=O\!\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)
.

2
2

2

The second statement follows from the observation that
\mathsf{Kac}^{\dagger}
contains enough steps of the parallel Kac’s walk.

In this work, we will prove that adding one more step of parallel Kac’s walk results in a distribution that is close to Haar random.
Our main result is:

Theorem 4.2

For
T=30n
and
d=5n
,
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T+1}
is computationally indistinguishable from Haar distribution.

We view the procedure of sampling a unitary from
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T+1}
as three stages:

1.

sample a unitary operator
\mathsf{Kac}
from
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
;

2.

sample a unitary operator
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}
from
\mathsf{HP}_{n,1}
corresponding to a random permutation
\sigma\in S_{N}
and a random function
f:\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{3d}
;

3.

output
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}\cdot\mathsf{Kac}
.

The proof of this theorem then consists of three steps:

•

We begin by establishing a purification of the adversary’s view state when making queries to the random unitary
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}
.
Specifically, we introduce two environment registers,

\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{P}
,
which record the function
f
and the permutation
\sigma
being utilized.
From the adversary’s perspective,
making queries to
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}
is equivalent to
querying a
purified function-permutation oracle

\mathsf{HPO}

that acts on both the adversary’s registers and the environment registers.

•

Next,
we demonstrate that
as long as
G
is a random unitary
sampled from an
RSS
distribution,
the adversary cannot distinguish between
making queries to
\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G

and making queries to a path-recording oracle
\mathsf{PR}
,
where
\mathsf{PR}
is defined in
Definition
11
.
The key insight in this step is that
if the environment state resides in the distinct block subspace,
we can identify an isometry acting on the environment
that connects the behaviors of
\mathsf{HPO}
and
\mathsf{PR}
.
The random unitary
G
ensures that
the environment state is nearly within the distinct block subspace.

•

Let
U
be sampled from Haar distribution
\mu
.
Since both
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
and
\mu
are
RSS
distributions,
we can conclude that
oracle
\mathsf{HPO}\cdot\mathsf{Kac}
and
\mathsf{HPO}\cdot U
are both indistinguishable
from the oracle
\mathsf{PR}

to the adversary.
Thus,
\mathsf{HPO}\cdot\mathsf{Kac}
and
\mathsf{HPO}\cdot U
are indistinguishable from each other.
This implies that making queries to
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}\cdot\mathsf{Kac}

and
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}\cdot U
are indistinguishable as well.
These two unitary operators correspond to
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T+1}
and Haar distribution,
respectively.

In the following, we will introduce the purified function-permutation oracle
\mathsf{HPO}
in
Section
4.2
and the path-recording oracle in
Section
4.3
.
We then explain how to connect the actions of
\mathsf{HPO}
and
\mathsf{PR}

by introducing an isometry
\mathsf{Compress}
in
Section
4.4
.
Lastly, we prove the main result in
Section
4.5
.

4.2
The Purified Function-Permutation Oracle

To analyze the behavior of querying
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}
, we employ a purified oracle.

Definition 9

(Purified Function-Permutation Oracle)
.

The
purified function-permutation oracle

\mathsf{HPO}
is a unitary
on registers
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{P}
, where

•

\mathsf{H}
is a register with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}
spanned by the orthogonal states
\ket{f}
for all
f\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{3d}
,

•

\mathsf{P}
is a register with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}
spanned by the orthogonal states
\ket{\sigma}
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{N}
.

The unitary operator
\mathsf{HPO}
acts as

\mathsf{HPO}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AHP}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}\coloneq\left(H_{f}P_{\sigma}\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}={H_{f}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\sigma(x)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}\kern 5.0pt.

In the adversary’s view, querying
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}
and querying
\mathsf{HPO}
are identical in the following sense:

Fact 4.3
.

For any adversary
\mathcal{A}
holding the register
\mathsf{A}
, the following two oracles are perfectly indistinguishable:

•

Sample uniformly random
\sigma\in S_{N}
and
f:\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{3d}
.
On each query, apply
H_{f}P_{\sigma}
on register
\mathsf{A}
.

•

Initialize registers
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{P}
in the state

\ket{\phi_{\{\}}}\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{3d(n-1)}}}\sum_{f:\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{3d}}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{N!}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{N}}\ket{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}\kern 5.0pt.

On each query, apply
\mathsf{HPO}
on registers
\mathsf{A}
,
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{P}
.

Consider the view of an adversary
\mathcal{A}
who makes
t
queries to
the oracle
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}\cdot G
where
G
is an arbitrary unitary operator:

\rho_{0}\coloneq\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{f}P_{\sigma}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\right]

where the view state is

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\left(H_{f}P_{\sigma}G\right)_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}^{(i)}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\kern 5.0pt,

and the view of the same adversary
\mathcal{A}
who makes
t
queries to
the oracle
\mathsf{HPO}
and the unitary
G
:

\rho_{1}\coloneq\mathrm{Tr}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\left(\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}\right)

where the view state is

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\mathsf{HPO}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AHP}}}\cdot G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}^{(i)}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\ket{\phi_{\{\}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\kern 5.0pt,

Fact
4.3
states that
\rho_{0}=\rho_{1}
.
This enables us to analyze the purified state instead of the original mixed state.
The action of the purified function-permutation oracle on the purified state can be better understood by introducing
\mathsf{HP}
-relation states on registers
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{P}
.

\mathsf{HP}
-Relation States

We define the following relation states on
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{P}
.

Definition 10

(
\mathsf{HP}
-Relation States)
.

For
0\leq t\leq N
and a size-
t
relation

R=\left\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,(x_{t},y_{t})\right\}\in\mathfrak{R}_{t}
, we define

\ket{\phi_{R}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{3d(n-1)}(N-t)!}}\sum_{f,\sigma}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{t}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bra{y_{i}}H_{f}\ket{y_{i}^{\oplus b_{i}}}\delta_{y_{i}^{\oplus b_{i}}=\sigma(x_{i})}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}

where
x^{\oplus 0}=x
and
x^{\oplus 1}=\overline{x}
for any
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and
\delta_{y=x}
is an indicator that equals
1
iff strings
x
and
y
are identical in every coordinate.

For the set
\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{DB}}
of restricted relations,
the corresponding
relation states form an orthogonal basis,
whose proof is given in
Appendix
0.A
.

Lemma 5

\left\{\ket{\phi_{R}}\right\}_{R\in\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{DB}}}
forms a set of orthogonal vectors.

Action of
\mathsf{HPO}

Using the
\mathsf{HP}
-relation states, the action of the
\mathsf{HPO}
oracle is described by the following lemma.

Lemma 6

For
0\leq t\leq N
,
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and a size-
t
relation

R=\{(x_{1},y_{1}),\dots,

(x_{t},y_{t})\}\in\mathfrak{R}_{t}
, we have

\mathsf{HPO}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AHP}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\phi_{R}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-t}}\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{y}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\ket{\phi_{R\cup\left\{x,y\right\}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

Expanding the definitions, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{HPO}

{}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AHP}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\phi_{R}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{3d(n-1)}(N-t)!}}\sum_{f,\sigma}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{t}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\bra{y_{i}}H_{f}\ket{y_{i}^{\oplus b_{i}}}\delta_{y_{i}^{\oplus b_{i}}=\sigma(x_{i})}H_{f}\ket{\sigma(x)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}\,.

(9)

Note that for a fixed
f:\{0,1\}^{n-1}\to\{0,1\}^{3d}
, the matrix
H_{f}
can be expressed as

H_{f}=\sum_{y,y^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\bra{y}H_{f}\ket{y^{\prime}}\ket{y}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{y^{\prime}}

and
\bra{y}H_{f}\ket{y^{\prime}}=0
if
y
and
y^{\prime}
are not in the same block.
So we can write
H_{f}
as

H_{f}=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n},b\in\{0,1\}}\bra{y}H_{f}\ket{y^{\oplus b}}\ket{y}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{y^{\oplus b}}\kern 5.0pt.

And we can write
\ket{\sigma(x)}
as
\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\delta_{y=\sigma(x)}\ket{y}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle H_{f}\ket{\sigma(x)}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n},b\in\{0,1\}}\bra{y}H_{f}\ket{y^{\oplus b}}\ket{y}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{y^{\oplus b}}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\delta_{y=\sigma(x)}\ket{y}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{0,1\}^{n},b\in\{0,1\}}\bra{y}H_{f}\ket{y^{\oplus b}}\delta_{y^{\oplus b}=\sigma(x)}\ket{y}\kern 5.0pt.

Inserting this into
(
9
)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{HPO}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AHP}}}\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{\phi_{R}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}=~\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-t}}\sum_{y_{t+1}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{y_{t+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}

\displaystyle\quad\otimes\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{3d(n-1)}(N-t-1)!}}\sum_{f,\sigma}\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{t+1}}\prod_{i=1}^{t+1}\bra{y_{i}}H_{f}\lvert y_{i}^{\oplus b_{i}}\rangle\delta_{y_{i}^{\oplus b_{i}}=\sigma(x_{i})}\ket{f}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}\ket{\sigma}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}

\displaystyle=~\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-t}}\sum_{y_{t+1}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\ket{y_{t+1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\otimes\ket{\phi_{R\cup\left\{x,y_{t+1}\right\}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\kern 5.0pt.\qed

By expanding
\mathsf{HPO}
, we can then rewrite the view state of an adversary
\mathcal{A}

with access to
the oracle
\mathsf{HPO}
and the unitary
G
in terms of
\mathsf{HP}
-relation states:

Corollary 1

For an arbitrary
n
-qubit unitary operator
G
and a
t
-query
adversary
\mathcal{A}
with query access to
\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G
, define the state after
\mathcal{A}
has finished all the queries to be

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\mathsf{HPO}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AHP}}}\cdot G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}^{(i)}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\ket{\phi_{\{\}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Then we have:

\displaystyle\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=\sqrt{\frac{(N-t)!}{N!}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})\in\left(\{0,1\}^{n}\right)^{t}\\
(y_{1},\dots,y_{t})\in\left(\{0,1\}^{n}\right)^{t}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\ket{y_{i}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{x_{i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}^{(i)}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\ket{\phi_{\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\kern 4.62497pt.

4.3
The Path-Recording Oracle

\mathsf{PR}

We now present the definition of our new path-recording oracle
\mathsf{PR}
,
which is similar to that in
[
22
]
.
The difference is that each time we query on
x
,
our oracle
\mathsf{PR}
samples a
y\notin\mathrm{BIm}\left(R\right)

whose block differs from the blocks of all previous
y
’s in
R
.

Definition 11

(Path-Recording Oracle)
.

The path-recording oracle
\mathsf{PR}
is a linear map

\mathsf{PR}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{A}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{Y}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{A}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{X}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{Y}}

defined as follows. For all
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
R\in\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{DB}}
,

\mathsf{PR}:\ket{x}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{R}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{XY}}}\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-2\left|R\right|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\{0,1\}^{n},\\
y\notin\mathrm{BIm}\left(R\right)\end{subarray}}\ket{y}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\ket{R\cup\left\{(x,y)\right\}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{XY}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Fact 4.4
.

For an
n
-qubit unitary operator
G
and a
t
-query
adversary
\mathcal{A}
with query access to
\mathsf{PR}\cdot G
, define the state after
\mathcal{A}
has finished all the queries to be

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\coloneq\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\mathsf{PR}\cdot G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A^{(i)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\ket{\{\}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{XY}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Then we have:

\displaystyle\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt=\sqrt{\prod_{i=0}^{t-1}\frac{1}{(N-2i)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})\in\left(\{0,1\}^{n}\right)^{t}\\
(y_{1},\dots,y_{t})\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\ket{y_{i}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{x_{i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}^{(i)}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\ket{{\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{XY}}}\kern 4.62497pt.

Similar to
[
22
, Lemma 4.3]
, our new path-recording oracle has the
right invariance
property, whose proof is given in
Appendix
0.B
.

Lemma 7

(Right invariance property)

For an arbitrary
n
-qubit unitary operator
G
, we have that

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}=\left(G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1}}}}\otimes\dots\otimes G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{t}}}}\right)\cdot\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Moreover, if the oracle
\mathsf{PR}
is preceded by a random scrambler and a random permutation, then the adversary’s record lies almost entirely in the desired
distinct block subspace
.

Lemma 8

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a distribution over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
such that
for
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}
,
K^{\dagger}
is an

\epsilon
-
RSS
with
\epsilon=O\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)
.
Define
\mathcal{D}
to be the distribution that samples
G=PK
where

P\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}
is a random permutation unitary and
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}
.
Let

\rho^{\mathcal{D}}\coloneq\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right]\kern 5.0pt,

then

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right)\geq 1-O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

By
Lemma
7
, we can rewrite
\rho^{\mathcal{D}}
as

\displaystyle\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}

\displaystyle=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1}}}}\otimes\dots\otimes G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{t}}}}\right)\cdot\ket{A_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{A_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\cdot\left(G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1}}}}\otimes\dots\otimes G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{t}}}}\right)^{\dagger}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Then by
Lemma
3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right)

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\left(G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1}}}}\otimes\dots\otimes G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{t}}}}\right)\cdot\ket{A_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{A_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\cdot\left(G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{1}}}}\otimes\dots\otimes G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X_{t}}}}\right)^{\dagger}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\geq~

\displaystyle 1-O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt.\qed

4.4
Connecting
\mathsf{HPO}
and
\mathsf{PR}
via
\mathsf{Compress}
Isometry

By defining the following isometry,
we are able to connect the behavior of
the purified function-permutation oracle
\mathsf{HPO}
and
the path-recording oracle
\mathsf{PR}
.

Definition 12
.

We define an isometry
\mathsf{Compress}:\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{P}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{H}}}\to\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{Y}}}
as:

\mathsf{Compress}:=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{DB}}}\ket{R}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\phi_{R}}\kern 5.0pt.

Lemma 9

Define the projector onto the
distinct block subspace
of
\mathsf{HP}
-relation states by

\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}:=\sum_{R\in\mathfrak{R}_{t}^{\mathsf{DB}}}\ket{\phi_{R}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\phi_{R}}\kern 5.0pt.

We have that for every
n
-qubit unitary
G
,

\mathsf{Compress}\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}=\left(1+O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\right)\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

By
Corollary
1
, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Compress}\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt=\sqrt{\frac{(N-t)!}{N!}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}\\
(y_{1},\dots,y_{t})\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\ket{y_{i}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{x_{i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}^{(i)}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\ket{{\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\kern 4.62497pt.

By
Fact
4.4
, we have

\displaystyle\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt=\sqrt{\prod_{i=0}^{t-1}\frac{1}{(N-2i)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}\\
(y_{1},\dots,y_{t})\in\mathsf{DB}_{t}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\ket{y_{i}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{x_{i}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot G_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{A}}}\cdot A_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}^{(i)}\right)\ket{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{AB}}}\ket{{\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{XY}}}\kern 4.62497pt.

Therefore, we observe that

\mathsf{Compress}\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}=\rho\cdot\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}

where

\rho=\sqrt{\prod_{i=0}^{t-1}\frac{N-i}{N-2i}}=\sqrt{\prod_{i=0}^{t-1}\left(1+\frac{i}{N-2i}\right)}=1+O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt.\qed

Using the above lemma, we show that
for a suitably sampled unitary
G
,
an adversary’s views before and after applying the projector
\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}
are close.

Lemma 10

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a distribution over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
such that
for
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}
,
K^{\dagger}
is an

\epsilon
-
RSS
with
\epsilon=O\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)
.
Define
\mathcal{D}
to be a distribution that samples
G=SK
where

S\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}
and
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
t
-query oracle adversary.
Write

\sigma\coloneq\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Then we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{HP}}\!\left(\sigma\right)-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{HP}}\!\left(\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\sigma\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\right)\right\rVert_{1}=O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We first apply
Lemma
1
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{HP}}\!\left(\sigma\right)-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{HP}}\!\left(\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\sigma\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\right)\right\rVert_{1}=1-\mathrm{Tr}\!\left(\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\sigma\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Notice that
\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}=\mathsf{Compress}^{\dagger}\cdot\mathsf{Compress}\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{HP}}\left(\sigma\right)-\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{HP}}\!\left(\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\sigma\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\right)\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle 1-\mathrm{Tr}\!\left(\mathsf{Compress}\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}\right]\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\mathsf{Compress}^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle 1-\left(1+O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\right)\mathrm{Tr}\!\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right]\right)=O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where the second equality uses
Lemma
9
and the last uses
Lemma
8
.
∎

4.5
Computational Indistinguishability of
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T+1}

We first show that if
G
consists of an RSS and a random permutation,
then the view of an adversary when making queries to
H_{f}P_{\sigma}G

is nearly the view it will see when making queries to the path-recording oracle
\mathsf{PR}
.

Lemma 11

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a distribution over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)
such that
for
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}
,
K^{\dagger}
is an

\epsilon
-
RSS
with
\epsilon=O\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)
.
Define
\mathcal{D}
to be a distribution that samples
G=SK
where

S\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}
and
K\leftarrow\mathcal{R}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
t
-query oracle adversary.
Then

\mathsf{TD}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{f}P_{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{HP}_{n,1},G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}G}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}G}}\right],\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\left(\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right)\right)=O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 4.62497pt.

Proof.

We start by defining the following states:

\displaystyle\rho_{0}

\displaystyle=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{f}P_{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{HP}_{n,1},G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}G}}\right]

\displaystyle\rho_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{HP}}\!\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\rho_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{HP}}\!\left(\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{HPO}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABHP}}}\right]\cdot\widetilde{\Pi}^{(t)}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{HP}}}\right)

\displaystyle\rho_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\!\left(\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\cdot\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right]\cdot\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}^{(t)}\right)

\displaystyle\rho_{4}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\!\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right]\right)\qquad\rho_{5}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\!\left(\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right)

By
Fact
4.3
,
\rho_{0}=\rho_{1}
.
By
Lemma
10
,
\left\lVert\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\right\rVert_{1}\leq O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)
.
By
Lemma
9
and the fact that
\mathsf{Compress}
acts only on the environment register, we have that
\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\rho_{3}\right\rVert_{1}=O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)
.
By
Lemma
8
and
Lemma
1
,
\left\lVert\rho_{3}-\rho_{4}\right\rVert_{1}\leq O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\rho_{4}=~

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\left({\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}\cdot G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\left(G^{\otimes t}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\cdot\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\cdot G^{\otimes t,{\dagger}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{X}}}\right)\right]=~\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\!\left(\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}\hskip-3.6999pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right)=~\rho_{5}\kern 4.62497pt,

where the second equality follows from
Lemma
7
.
Combining the above bounds with the triangle inequality proves the lemma.
∎

We now prove that the distribution
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T+1}
is computationally indistinguishable from Haar distribution.

Proof of
Theorem
4.2
.

Consider an adversary
\mathcal{A}
who makes
t=\mathrm{poly}\!\left(n\right)
queries.
Note that the distribution of
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}\cdot\mathsf{Kac}

is the same as the distribution of
H_{f}\cdot P_{\sigma}\cdot S\cdot\mathsf{Kac}
where we add a random permutation matrix
S\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}
.
Therefore,

\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}}}\right]=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{f}P_{\sigma}S\mathsf{Kac}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}S\mathsf{Kac}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}S\mathsf{Kac}}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\mathsf{Kac}
is sampled from
\mathsf{HP}_{n,T}
and
S\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}
, by
Theorem
4.1
and
Lemma
11
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}}}\right],\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\left(\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right)\right)=O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Now substitute
\mathsf{Kac}
with a Haar random unitary
U
and we have

\displaystyle\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{U}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{U}}\right]=\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{f}P_{\sigma}SU\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}SU}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}SU}}\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Since
U
is a
0
-
RSS
and
S\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{N}
, we have that by
Lemma
11

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{U}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{U}}\right],\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathsf{XY}}\left(\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{ABXY}}}\right)\right)=O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Then by the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{TD}\left(\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{U}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{U}}\right],\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}\end{subarray}}\!\left[\ket{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}}}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{\mathcal{A}_{t}^{H_{f}P_{\sigma}\mathsf{Kac}}}\right]\right)=O\!\left(\frac{t^{2}}{N}\right)=\mathrm{negl}\!\left(n\right)\kern 5.0pt.

This proves the theorem.
∎

Our construction of
PRU
based on parallel
Kac’s walk is to use QPRF and QPRP when sampling from
\widehat{\mathsf{HP}}_{n,T+1}
.

Theorem 4.5

By replacing random functions and random permutations with
their post-quantum secure pseudorandom counterparts in the sampling procedure of
\widehat{\mathsf{HP}}_{n,T+1}
where
T=30n
and
d=5n
, we obtain a
PRU
.

Proof.

By the post-quantum security of QPRP and QPRF,

Lemma
4
and
Theorem
4.2
, the new distribution is computationally indistinguishable from Haar distribution.
This new distribution can be sampled in polynomial time
since QPRP and QPRF can be sampled efficiently. ∎

A closer look at our analysis actually reveals a more general and abstract statement. Namely, given a distribution over unitaries, we identify a property which approximates Haar random unitary under a specific twirling operation. As soon as this property holds, composing it with a single step of the parallel Kac’s walk would produce a PRU. This readily implies a number of instantiations and can help mechanize the design of PRU.

Theorem 4.6

Let
V
be an
n
-qubit unitary sampled from the distribution
\mathsf{HP}_{n,1}
(i.e., a single step of parallel Kac’s walk). Let
\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}:=\sum_{x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}}\ket{x,x}\hskip-3.99994pt\bra{x,x}
. Suppose a unitary
K
is sampled from a distribution
\mathcal{R}
over
\mathrm{U}\!\left(N\right)

such that

\left\lVert\E_{\begin{subarray}{c}K\end{subarray}}\!\left[(K\otimes K)^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{\mathsf{eq}}\cdot(K\otimes{K})\right]\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\delta\kern 5.0pt.

Then, for any adversary making at most
t
queries,

VK
is indistinguishable from a Haar random unitary except with advantage at most
O(t^{2}\delta+t^{2}/N)
. Some examples of instantiations of
K
are below.

•

K
is a
\delta
-approximate unitary
2
-design, or

•

K^{\dagger}
is an
O(\delta/N)
-
RSS
. For instance, for
K\leftarrow\mathsf{HP}_{n,T=30n}
,
K^{\dagger}
has the same distribution, and hence satisfies it.

5
Showing the Strong Security of

\mathsf{HP}_{n,2T+1}

We further show that our construction, based on Kac’s walk, also achieves strong security
when adversaries are granted query access to the inverse unitary.

Theorem 5.1

(
\mathsf{HP}_{n,2T+1}
is a statistical strong-
PRU
)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
t
-query oracle adversary
performing both forward and inverse queries to oracle
\mathcal{O}
, and let

\mathsf{HP}_{n,2T+1}
be defined as in
Definition
8
with
T=30n
and
d=5n
. Then
